  <main class="plan-doc">
    <h1>Work Does Not Fall To The Coordinator — Landed Evidence</h1>

    <section id="the-landing-comment-names-its-writer">
      <h2>The landing comment names its writer</h2>
      <p><code>reckon/crew/promotion.py</code> now attributes a comment written by
      promotion to <code>reckon-ship</code>, while the existing committed-record
      check continues to preserve <code>worker-a</code> when the worker actually
      authored the plan comment. A repeated run id is idempotent only when its
      stored body is unchanged; a different narrative now refuses explicitly
      rather than reporting that stale text was recorded.</p>

      <table>
        <thead>
          <tr><th>Measure</th><th>Before</th><th>After</th></tr>
        </thead>
        <tbody>
          <tr>
            <td>Coordinator-written <code>who</code></td>
            <td><code>worker-a</code></td>
            <td><code>reckon-ship</code></td>
          </tr>
          <tr>
            <td>Same id, changed narrative</td>
            <td>Unqualified success; stale body retained</td>
            <td>Explicit refusal; stale body left untouched</td>
          </tr>
          <tr>
            <td>Focused falsifiers and controls</td>
            <td>2 failed, 3 passed</td>
            <td>5 passed</td>
          </tr>
          <tr>
            <td>Required cohort on <code>all_debug</code></td>
            <td>22 failed, 303 passed</td>
            <td>22 failed, 308 passed; 0 added failure ids</td>
          </tr>
        </tbody>
      </table>

      <p>The unchanged-body retry still writes nothing, and the fourth attempt
      still succeeds after three consecutive version conflicts. Every fixture
      uses a temporary repository and temporary crew home, with assertions that
      the repository's real docs paths and crew home receive no fixture record.
      The base and repaired cohorts were captured separately on the same SDCC
      debug partition after a login-node run exposed an unrelated live process
      at a fixture's synthetic PID; both completed on compute and returned the
      same 22 pre-existing failure ids.
      Implementation: <code>7bf1f8d662e70522dac9d38ea8c8fd1d2cafec05</code>.</p>
    </section>
  <section id="refusal-orders-before-the-write">
    <h2>§2 — A promotion that is going to refuse leaves the plan as it found it</h2>

    <p><strong>Source ordering confirmed before repair.</strong> In
    <code>reckon/crew/promotion.py</code>, <code>_record_landing_comment</code> was called at line
    2808 — it writes the landing comment into the plan HTML and bumps
    <code>plan-version</code> — and <code>ledger.build_record</code> at line 2858, whose
    gate-evidence guard raises from <code>reckon/ledger.py:1133</code> with
    <em>“a passing gate requires the check that produced it; missing command, exit_status, log
    path or digest”</em>. Fifty lines separate the mutation from the check that rejects the call,
    and none of that check needs the write to have happened.</p>

    <img src="/reckon/figures/refused-promotion/ordering.png"
         alt="Two orderings: before, the plan comment is written before the ledger build that refuses; after, every check precedes the write."
         style="max-width:100%">

    <table>
      <thead><tr><th>Measure</th><th>Base <code>09615744</code></th><th>Landed</th><th>Delta</th></tr></thead>
      <tbody>
        <tr>
          <td>Focused falsifiers and controls</td>
          <td>3 failed, 3 passed</td>
          <td>6 passed</td>
          <td>3 falsifiers reproduce and repair; 0 added</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
          <td><code>test_gate_evidence.py</code></td>
          <td>10 failed, 16 passed</td>
          <td>10 failed, 16 passed</td>
          <td>0 added, same 10 pre-existing ids</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
          <td><code>test_landing_comment_authorship.py</code></td>
          <td>5 passed</td>
          <td>5 passed</td>
          <td>0 added</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
          <td><code>test_ledger.py</code></td>
          <td>collected 95; stalls around 80</td>
          <td>collected 95; stalls at the same point</td>
          <td>pre-existing stall, not attributable</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
          <td>Cohort of the four files, combined</td>
          <td>13 failed, 24 passed</td>
          <td>10 failed, 27 passed</td>
          <td>3 failures repaired; the 10 remaining are the same pre-existing ids</td>
        </tr>
      </tbody>
    </table>

    <p>The three falsifiers in <code>tests/test_refused_promotion_leaves_no_trace.py</code> each
    reproduce the recorded defect at base and pass after the repair:</p>

    <ul>
      <li>A missing gate-evidence refusal left the plan file rewritten and <code>plan-version</code>
      moved <code>0 → 1</code>; after, the file is byte-identical, the version is unmoved, and the
      pointer survives for a retry.</li>
      <li>The range case: an off-vocabulary gate verdict, refused inside the same record build,
      likewise left a written plan at base and leaves none after — so the repair is the ordering,
      not one message being moved.</li>
      <li>The retry case: at base the refused attempt had already written the comment, so the
      retry met a stale narrative; after, the refused attempt moves neither the plan bytes, the
      version, nor <code>HEAD</code>, and the immediately following retry lands with no
      intervening commit or cleanup.</li>
    </ul>

    <p>Positive controls hold: a well-formed promotion still writes exactly one landing comment
    and bumps <code>plan-version</code> once; a second promotion of an already-landed run reports
    both that it was already promoted and that the comment was already recorded, writing nothing;
    and a worker-authored landing record still declines to duplicate, with the narrative promotion
    then kept on the ledger row rather than dropped.</p>

    <p><strong>Repair by order, not by guard.</strong> The record build and every refusal it raises
    move above the comment write, with the one coupling preserved: the row carries its outcome
    empty when the comment recorded the narrative, and the ledger holds the row in memory until
    the comment's own decision is known. No fixture reaches the real crew home, and the
    assertions on the checkout's own <code>docs/plans/</code> and <code>docs/state/</code> paths
    hold before and after the run.</p>
  </section>


    <section id="s2-roster-loss-guard">
      <h2>§2 — A ledger write never drops a member it was not asked to drop.

      <p><strong>Defect guarded.</strong> A ledger write that replaces the roster
      with fewer members than the stored version holds is how a roster empties
      itself through a write meant for something else: the write succeeds, the
      commit reports success, and the loss surfaces later as a dispatch refusing
      a member the coordinator registered moments before. The best available
      reproduction is the versioned write path: the guard compares the member
      id set of the incoming write against the ledger version it replaces and
      refuses when an id the stored roster holds is left out, naming the dropped
      ids and the intent flag that permits a real removal.</p>

      <p><strong>Identity, not count.</strong> The comparison is by member id set
      rather than by member count, because a same-count swap loses an id while
      the count reports nothing missing. A swap is refused by the same rule that
      refuses a drop.</p>

      <table>
        <thead><tr><th>Measure</th><th>Base <code>44a46be8</code></th><th>Landed</th><th>Delta</th></tr></thead>
        <tbody>
          <tr><td>Falsifiers: drop-to-zero, drop-of-one, drop-two-add-one, same-count swap, missing <code>members</code> key</td><td>no refusal exists; the write empties the roster</td><td>5 refused, roster byte-unchanged after each</td><td>guard fires on the whole domain, not just 87&rarr;0</td></tr>
          <tr><td>Positive controls: new registration, re-registration, member-preserving promotion write, absent ledger created from empty</td><td>pass</td><td>pass</td><td>the guard stays quiet where it should</td></tr>
          <tr><td>Explicit removal with <code>allow_member_removal=True</code> (one member and all members)</td><td>n/a</td><td>succeeds</td><td>a genuine removal stays possible and is stated at the call site</td></tr>
          <tr><td>Focused suite <code>tests/test_roster_loss_guard.py</code></td><td>n/a (new file)</td><td>14 passed</td><td>13 guard cases plus the reaper's granted intent</td></tr>
          <tr><td>Cohort: <code>test_ledger.py</code>, <code>test_roster_write_commits.py</code>, <code>test_crew_members.py</code></td><td>1 failed / 94 passed; 3 passed; 3 failed</td><td>1 failed / 94 passed; 3 passed; 3 failed</td><td>0 added failure ids, same ids both trees</td></tr>
        </tbody>
      </table>

      <p><strong>The one production caller entitled to the removal.</strong>
      <code>reap idle-members</code> filters the roster down to the surviving rows
      and writes it back &mdash; a deliberate removal and the whole purpose of the
      reaper, which retires idle session-owned rows while retaining their run
      history. It is the single caller that declares
      <code>allow_member_removal=True</code>; every other caller passes nothing
      and has its unrequested decrease refused. Reproduction in both directions
      with one idle <code>session-abc</code> member in a temp checkout and a temp
      configuration home:</p>
      <table>
        <thead><tr><th>Tree</th><th>Reap result</th><th>Roster after</th></tr></thead>
        <tbody>
          <tr><td>Base <code>44a46be8</code></td><td>reaped <code>session-abc</code></td><td><code>[]</code></td></tr>
          <tr><td>Guard without the intent flag</td><td>raised after 12 attempts; dispatch fails loud</td><td><code>['session-abc']</code></td></tr>
          <tr><td>Landed <code>56953220</code></td><td>reaped <code>session-abc</code>, active member kept</td><td><code>['clive-1']</code></td></tr>
        </tbody>
      </table>

      <p>Every fixture is a throwaway checkout under a temporary directory with
      <code>RECKON_HOME</code> pointed at a temporary tree; the closing test
      asserts the refusal left the ledger byte-identical, the temp tree
      unchanged, and the configuration home outside the real one. Product paths:
      <code>reckon/ledger.py</code> (the guard and its <code>dropped_member_ids</code>
      helper) and <code>reckon/crew/routing.py</code> (the reaper's declared
      intent). Regression coverage: <code>tests/test_roster_loss_guard.py</code>.
      Logs are recorded in the node manifest.</p>
    </section>
  <section id="the-commit-guard-asks-about-repository-paths">
    <h2>§2 — The commit guard asks about repository paths</h2>

    <p><strong>Defect reproduced before repair.</strong> A run whose manifest was
    <code>status: complete</code>, whose <code>commits</code> field was correctly empty, and whose
    <code>changed_paths</code> named only absolute paths outside its repository was refused by
    <code>_require_commit_for_changed_manifest</code> in <code>reckon/crew/promotion.py</code> with
    <em>“has a complete manifest with changed_paths, but the manifest field 'commits' is
    missing”</em>. That is the shape of a review or investigate run: its deliverable lands under
    the crew reviews directory, so there is nothing inside any repository to commit and the run
    could never be reconciled. At base the outside-only case raised that error and failed.</p>

    <p>The guard now asks whether any named path resolves under the run's own repository root: a
    relative path is repository-relative, an absolute path resolves as written, and a path
    anywhere else — a store directory, a scratch path under the crew home — requires no commit
    here. A run whose paths all lie outside its repository therefore promotes.</p>

    <svg viewBox="0 0 900 420" role="img"
         aria-label="Decision flow: a complete manifest with changed_paths and no commits refuses only when a named path resolves under the run's repository root; otherwise it promotes."
         style="max-width:100%;height:auto">

        </marker>
      </defs>

  <section id="unreconciled-grace-clears-the-promotion-beat">
    <h2>§7 — The unreconciled-run grace clears the measured promotion beat</h2>

    <p><strong>The shipped grace was shorter than the task it exists to accommodate.</strong>
    <code>fences.unreconciled_run_grace</code> shipped as <code>5m</code> in
    <code>reckon/schema/flight-defaults.yaml</code>, the bottom resolution layer. Three fleets
    report a two-to-six minute promotion beat, and a refill refusal fired mid-beat: a
    coordinator merges a landed branch, verifies the marker at the merged head and promotes,
    which is the work the fence exists to force rather than a backlog it exists to surface. The
    declared default is now <code>15m</code> — nine minutes of margin over the slowest observed
    beat, and an abandoned pile still surfaces within a quarter hour.</p>

    <svg viewBox="0 0 900 260" role="img"
         aria-label="Timeline: measured promotion beats occupy two to six minutes; the old five-minute grace falls inside that band so the refusal fired mid-beat, while the new fifteen-minute grace clears it with nine minutes of margin."

        <text x="700" y="92" text-anchor="middle" fill="#555">15 min</text>

        <line x1="460" y1="110" x2="460" y2="170" stroke="#a11" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>

        <rect x="340" y="212" width="620" height="34" rx="6" fill="#e8f6ec" stroke="#4a9a63"/>
        <text x="650" y="234" text-anchor="middle" fill="#0a6b2d">refusal reserved for a run abandoned past the grace</text>
      </g>

    <p><strong>Reproduced at base, cleared by the change.</strong> The focused test resolves the
    shipped layer with the host and project layers absent — the state a fresh install runs with
    — and converts the value with the same parser dispatch compares a pointer's age against.
    At base <code>7dfae503</code> the resolved grace was <strong>300</strong> seconds against
    the required 900, so the default case failed as expected.</p>

    <table>
      <thead><tr><th>Case</th><th>Base <code>7dfae503</code></th><th>Landed? </th></tr></thead>
      <tbody>
        <tr><td>shipped default, no layer overriding it</td><td>2 failed, 80 passed, 2 skipped</td><td>82 passed, 2 skipped</td></tr>
      </tbody>
    </table>

    <p>The two failures at base were the falsifiers: <code>parse_duration('5m')</code> is 300,
    not 900. Controls in the same file pass at both revisions — a project layer tightening the
    grace to <code>3m</code> wins over the shipped value, a caller override of <code>90s</code>
    wins over the project layer, and the shipped value matches the pattern its slot declares.</p>

    <p><strong>The generated artifacts are unchanged, and that is provable rather than assumed.
    </strong> The generators read <code>reckon/schema/flight.yaml</code> alone;
    <code>reckon/schema/flight-defaults.yaml</code> is the shipped resolution layer and is not
    an input to them, so a default change regenerates nothing. <code>git status</code> after
    the edit lists only the defaults file, the new test and the shipped-value assertion in
    <code>tests/test_flight.py</code>: neither <code>reckon/_flight_schema.py</code> nor
    <code>docs/_shared/flight.schema.json</code> moved. The round-trip tests that would prove
    that by regeneration are skipped on this host because <code>gen-pydantic</code> and
    <code>gen-json-schema</code> are absent.</p>

    <p>Because those two are skipped here, the new test carries its own agreement check that
    reads the LinkML source with no generator toolchain: it compares the slot's
    <code>pattern</code> against the compiled pattern in the committed pydantic model and the
    <code>pattern</code> and <code>type</code> in the committed JSON Schema. <strong>It was
    shown to fire rather than assumed to.</strong> Mutating the source's pattern to
    <code>^[0-9]+[smhx]$</code> and leaving the artifacts alone turned the check red with
    <code>AssertionError</code> at the JSON Schema comparison; reverting the source restored
    <code>git diff</code> to clean.</p>

    <p><strong>Gate.</strong> <code>PYTHONPATH=$PWD .venv/bin/python -m pytest -p
    no:cacheprovider -q tests/test_unreconciled_grace_default.py tests/test_flight.py</code> —
    base <code>2 failed, 80 passed, 2 skipped</code>, landed <code>82 passed, 2 skipped</code>,
    zero added failures. A wider cohort of the files that resolve or assert this fence —
    <code>tests/test_flight.py</code>, <code>tests/test_unreconciled_grace_default.py</code>,
    <code>tests/test_crew_drain_by_coordinator.py</code> and
    <code>tests/test_closure_fence_agreement.py</code> — is <code>92 passed, 2 skipped</code>.
    The latter two declare the fence in their own fixtures rather than reading the shipped
    layer, so they are a control on the change rather than a range over it.</p>

    <p><strong>The declared fence did not cover two of the files the change required, and that
    is a fence defect rather than a judgement call to bury.</strong>
    <code>reckon/schema/flight-defaults.yaml</code> is where the default is declared — the
    brief and the fence both name <code>reckon/schema/flight.yaml</code>, which carries no
    defaults by design — and <code>tests/test_flight.py</code> asserts the shipped grace by
    literal, so the node's own gate cannot be green without it. Two further fence paths,
    <code>reckon/crew/_flight_schema.py</code> and <code>reckon/flight.schema.json</code>,
    resolve to nothing; the real artifacts are <code>reckon/_flight_schema.py</code> and
    <code>docs/_shared/flight.schema.json</code>. The change was made in the files the intent
    required and both deviations are reported here and in the manifest.</p>
  </section>

  <main class="plan-doc">
    <h1>Work Does Not Fall To The Coordinator — Landed Evidence</h1>

    <section id="s2-claim-release">
      <h2>A write claim can be narrowed or released without a promotion</h2>
      <p>A run's declared write paths are created at dispatch and were, until this
      change, destroyed only at promotion. <code>--write-path</code> appears in the help
      of one of the 31 <code>crew</code> subcommands, <code>dispatch</code>, and the
      scan finds it there as its own positive control; no <code>crew claim</code>
      subcommand exists. A coordinator whose worker blocked at its fence therefore had
      two moves, both wrong: hand-edit the live pointer under the shared crew config
      home, or redispatch and discard every turn of context the worker held.</p>

      <p><code>reckon/crew/claims.py</code> (commit
      <code>c754cfe2a37fd5e4e25ab3523078804de99f0020</code>) adds
      <code>release_claim</code> and <code>narrow_claim</code>, which rewrite the
      pointer's <code>node.write_paths</code> and report the amendment as a
      <code>ClaimAmendment</code> — previous and current declarations, the paths
      removed, and any unintegrated work the stopped run still holds. Nothing is wired
      to a command: <code>reckon/cli.py</code> is owned by a second node, so the module
      is the deliverable and the intended entry point is
      <code>crew claim narrow|release</code>, rendering <code>ClaimAmendment.as_dict()</code>.</p>

      <h3>Refusal is the mechanism</h3>
      <p>An amendment is admitted only for a worker shown to have stopped, and "shown"
      is narrow by design: a live process refuses; a pointer whose
      <code>launcher_host</code> is not this host refuses, because a pid answers only
      on the machine that issued it and the crew config home is shared across login
      nodes; and a pointer that records no process yet refuses, because the pointer is
      written before its worker is spawned. Ones that cannot be answered refuse rather
      than being read as dead.</p>

      <table>
        <thead><tr><th>Measure</th><th>Base <code>6c577fa2</code></th><th>Landed</th><th>Delta</th></tr></thead>
        <tbody>
          <tr>
            <td>Release path present</td>
            <td><code>git cat-file -e …:reckon/crew/claims.py</code> exits 128</td>
            <td>module present; 10 focused tests pass</td>
            <td>the falsifier's precondition, absent before</td>
          </tr>
          <tr>
            <td>Claim check (<code>plan_scope_lanes</code>) after release</td>
            <td>no release is expressible</td>
            <td>a second writer declaring the path raises no live conflict</td>
            <td>the falsifier itself</td>
          </tr>
          <tr>
            <td><code>tests/test_crew.py</code> on <code>all_debug</code></td>
            <td>11 failed, 193 passed</td>
            <td>11 failed, 193 passed</td>
            <td>0 added failure ids, identical identifier set</td>
          </tr>
          <tr>
            <td>Three-file cohort</td>
            <td>10 failed, 24 passed</td>
            <td>10 failed, 24 passed</td>
            <td>0 added; all ten are in <code>test_crew_cross_project_claims.py</code></td>
          </tr>
        </tbody>
      </table>

      <p>The positive controls are the untouched claim that still refuses a second
      writer, and the pointer removal that releases the claim exactly as promotion
      does. The range covers a terminal run's release, a two-path claim narrowed to
      one, and a release attempted against a live process — which refuses and leaves
      the declaration intact — plus a foreign launching host, a pointer with no
      process, an undeclared path, and a narrowing that drops nothing. Fixtures use a
      temporary repository and a temporary crew home, and the fixture asserts the real
      crew config home is unchanged afterwards, because this module writes there. Both
      cohorts ran on <code>all_debug</code> with the whole run captured once to
      <code>/tmp/claim_gate_heavy.log</code> and
      <code>/tmp/claim_base_heavy.log</code>. Changed paths:
      <code>reckon/crew/claims.py</code> and <code>tests/test_claim_release.py</code>,
      both new; no existing file is modified.</p>
    </section>
  <section id="s2-dispatch-registers-commits">
    <h2>A dispatch commits the session member it registers for itself</h2>
    <p><strong>Defect reproduced before repair.</strong> A dispatch handed no member
    provisions one under its own session, and that registration path wrote the roster
    without requesting a commit. At base <code>7dfae503</code>, after a session
    registration succeeded, <code>git rev-list --count base..HEAD</code> was
    <code>0</code> and <code>git status --porcelain -- docs/state/proj/crew.json</code>
    reported the file untracked: the row existed only in the checkout that wrote it and
    rode whichever commit came next. A declared member's registration never sits in that
    state, because the registration code commits what it writes.</p>
    <p>Commit <code>6a0bb500</code> requests the commit at the call site that performs
    the write. <code>_register_session_member</code> passes <code>commit=True</code> to
    <code>ledger.register_member</code>, so the row reaches the repository as
    <code>chore(roster): register &lt;id&gt;</code> under a subject naming the member and a
    commit carrying only <code>docs/state/&lt;project&gt;/crew.json</code>. A commit that
    fails is surfaced as a <code>CrewError</code> naming the uncommitted registration
    instead of being retried into a registration the caller believes landed.</p>
    <p><strong>The committing registration exposed an ordering defect in the same path.</strong>
    A dispatch reaps idle session rows before it registers its own, so both roster writes
    land in one checkout in sequence. A reap left in the tree is content that is not the
    registration's to record, so the registration's commit must refuse it — and then the
    dispatch provisions no member at all. The reaper now commits the removal it writes,
    and skips that commit when the roster already carries content that is not its own,
    which leaves the write in the tree exactly as an unrequested write would and leaves the
    next named commit to refuse it. The skip is what keeps a roster whose checkout cannot
    answer, and a concurrent peer's uncommitted registration, from being absorbed.</p>

    <figure style="margin:1.2rem 0;">
      <img src="/reckon/figures/registration-commit/roster_write_order.png"
           alt="Left: the reap commits before the registration commits, and an uncommitted reap would be refused by the registration that follows it. Right: the four-file gate moves from ten failures at base to three pre-existing failures at the repair."
           style="width:100%;height:auto;">
      <figcaption>The order of the two roster writes a dispatch makes, and the gate delta.
      Panel A is the sequence the reaper's commit exists for: the registration commits the
      row it writes and refuses any roster content that is not its own, so a reap left
      uncommitted would refuse the registration and leave the dispatch with no member.
      Panel B: the seven falsifiers fail at base and pass at the repair, with the three
      pre-existing <code>test_crew_members.py</code> failures unchanged either side.</figcaption>
    </figure>

    <table>
      <thead><tr><th>Measure</th><th>Base <code>7dfae503</code></th><th>Repair <code>6a0bb500</code></th><th>Delta</th></tr></thead>
      <tbody>
        <tr><td>Four-file gate (<code>test_member_registration_commits.py</code>, <code>test_roster_write_commits.py</code>, <code>test_roster_loss_guard.py</code>, <code>test_crew_members.py</code>)</td><td>10 failed, 19 passed</td><td>3 failed, 26 passed</td><td>0 added failures; the 7 registrations falsifiers all fixed</td></tr>
        <tr><td>Pre-existing failures</td><td>3 (<code>test_crew_members.py</code>)</td><td>3, identical ids</td><td>unchanged</td></tr>
        <tr><td>A caller that does not ask to commit</td><td>write left untracked</td><td>write left untracked</td><td>positive control holds</td></tr>
        <tr><td>A commit that fails</td><td>registration reported as success</td><td>surfaced as <code>CrewError</code>, tree left for retry</td><td>the failure is named rather than hidden</td></tr>
      </tbody>
    </table>

    <p>The three remaining failures raise <em>spec-level: no specification level is
    declared</em> from <code>reckon/crew/dispatch.py:2787</code> and fail identically at base
    with the source unchanged; they sit outside this node's write scope and were not
    touched.</p>
    <p>Regression coverage: <code>tests/test_member_registration_commits.py</code>
    (nine tests, each building a throwaway repository and a temporary crew home). Changed
    product paths: <code>reckon/crew/routing.py</code> and <code>reckon/ledger.py</code>.</p>
  </section>

  <section id="manifest-check-reaches-the-writer">
    <h2>&sect;9 &mdash; The manifest check reaches its writer while the run still holds a turn</h2>

    <p><strong>The defect was a reader with no caller.</strong>
    <code>reckon/crew/reports.py:audit_manifest</code> already judged a delivered manifest
    against its comparison node and <em>returned</em> findings rather than raising, which is the
    shape a refusal addressed to a worker needs. On main every reference outside its own
    definition was the crew facade re-export at <code>reckon/crew.py:193</code> and four test
    modules: no production caller existed, so the only enforcement point was the promotion
    contract, which reads the same manifest hours after the worker's process has ended. A
    finding there reaches a coordinator, whose only remedy is editing an artifact it did not
    author — measured twice in imas-efit on 2026-09-21, with two coordinators produced two
    different repairs for the same field.</p>

    <svg viewBox="0 0 760 210" role="img" aria-label="Two arrival times for one manifest audit"

    <p>Commit <code>8196dc7f</code> adds <code>crew check-manifest</code> to
    <code>reckon/cli.py</code>. It reads the run's live pointer, rebuilds the node that pointer
    records, and reports the audit findings beside the run id it read:</p>

    <pre>crew check-manifest --run &lt;run-id&gt;

        <tr><td>Four-file cohort (the new file plus <code>test_manifest_parse_format.py</code>, <code>test_crew_surface_parity.py</code>, <code>test_removed_flag_migration.py</code>)</td><td>2 failed, 118 passed; both failures outside this node's scope (below)</td></tr>
        <tr><td>Production call sites of <code>audit_manifest</code> after the change</td><td>exactly three files: <code>reckon/cli.py</code> (the caller), <code>reckon/crew.py</code> (facade re-export), <code>reckon/crew/reports.py</code> (the definition)</td></tr>
      </tbody>

    <h3>The mutation, and that the uncovered behaviour is real</h3>
    <p>The declared mutation drops the node argument at the call site, so the audit never
    compares the manifest's paths against the node's write fence. Applied with the write
    scope's own tool to a copy taken first, and the red log captured to the node's run directory
    with the declaration verbatim on its first line; the source was then restored from the copy
    and diffed byte-identical against it. What the mutation uncovers is not a hypothetical API
    misuse: a manifest naming a path the node could never have written is reported clean and
    exits zero — which is the behaviour a conformance check would fail on, and it is what let the
    coordinator's hand edit pass without comment.</p>

    <h3>Two failures found outside this node's scope</h3>
    <p><code>tests/test_removed_flag_migration.py</code> reports none of its follower
    transitions — one case delivered no baseline row, another never received a transition, both
    timing out. They are reported rather than triaged or fixed, because they name no path this
    node declared. The one link that would have made them reachable was checked before saying
    so: the same argv the test spawns, run directly against a scratch crew home, starts and
    prints its deprecation notice on stderr, so the CLI the tests launch is sound. The file fails
    identically alone (2 failed, 4 passed).</p>

    <p>Changed product paths: <code>reckon/cli.py</code> and
    <code>tests/test_manifest_check_command.py</code>. The negative control is
    <code>negative-control.txt</code> in the node's run directory.</p>
  </section>

  <section id="the-composer-reads-the-report-a-run-delivered">
    <h2>§4 — A landed record composes from the report a run delivered</h2>

    <p><strong>The gap.</strong> The composer had one source: the ledger row. A row whose
    run landed no commits carries no <code>commits</code>, no <code>tests_added</code> and
    no <code>changed_lines</code>, because an investigate node's deliverable is a report on
    disk rather than a diff — so <code>_run_rows</code> rendered a row of dashes and the
    section was empty. That is the state in which a coordinator authors the evidence record
    by hand, writing the evidence for work it is also judging. This section records the
    second source: the report the run itself delivered.</p>

    <p>Commit <code>1f12f4bf</code> adds a report-backed source to
    <code>reckon/evidence.py</code> and nothing else. The ledger row already carries
    <code>manifest_path</code>; the run's manifest names the durable paths it delivered; and
    the composer now reads that manifest through the existing
    <code>reckon.crew.reports.parse_manifest</code> rather than through a second reader, so
    the manifest contract stays declared in one place. A run whose deliverable is readable is
    cited by path with its content reproduced under
    <code>&lt;article class="landed-report"&gt;</code>; a run that landed no commits and
    named no readable report is stated as such under
    <code>landed-report-unreadable</code>, because an anchored section with no content reads
    as work that landed nothing rather than as a record that could not be composed.</p>

    <figure>
      <svg viewBox="0 0 960 300" width="960" height="300" role="img"
